# Part 1–2 evidence ledger, framing batch (Claude Code session)

**Date:** 2026-09-30 · **Session:** Claude Code · **Scope:** the evidence behind `notes/part1/2026-09-30-how-the-literature-defines-agent-acceleration-zh.md`: how each paper in the deck reference list and in the Part 3 store states its problem, the bottleneck it names, its objective, its solution logic and its claimed gain ("framing records"). New IDs continue the repository maxima E1313 and D594: E1314–E1675 here; no D-entries were drafted for this batch (no conflict with the ledger was checked; within-source notes stay in the records' notes fields).

## How the evidence was produced

1. **Corpus.** (a) The deck reference lists REFS_P1 and REFS_P2 in `slides/build_deck.py` (114 items after de-duplication; 98 papers, of which 13 already had audited records in the store and 85 were fetched on 2026-09-30 as records p001–p085; 16 vendor pricing pages, product documents and similar are outside the corpus); (b) the 378 works of the Part 3 store with saved full texts (batches w, n, g, h, x, s; the two SAP product pages excluded). 463 framing records, 432 distinct works.
2. **Extraction.** One reader per batch of four works read the abstract, the introduction and the problem-formulation or preliminaries section of the saved text and wrote one framing record per work: problem as stated, named challenge, objective (formula copied with symbols where the paper writes one), solution principle, claimed gain, each locked to a line number with a verbatim anchor of at most twelve words; plus a reader classification (is efficiency central, secondary or absent in the stated problem; the paper's own efficiency terms; agent kind; position in the pipeline).
3. **Mechanical check.** `tools/evidence-check/check_framing.py` verified every anchor against its line (±2): 463 records, 2,311 anchored fields, 0 problems.
4. **Audit.** A second reader, who did not write the record, tried to refute each field against the saved text and corrected or rejected the record. All 463 records were audited: 231 by Claude audit agents (all 85 deck records, 124 store records in the first pass and the 22 cited store records in a second pass) and, after Edwin asked that the Claude quota be kept for the main session, 232 by Codex (`gpt-6-astra`, reasoning effort ultra, five tasks in parallel, ten records each) under the same written rules (`codex-audit/PROMPT.md` in the store). Codex rejected 25 records because the source is not a research paper (company blog posts, product documentation, READMEs, pricing pages); they are excluded from the records file and are not cited. The Audit column of every item names the auditor (claude or codex). After the audits `check_framing.py` was re-run: 2,314 anchored fields, 0 problems.
5. **Source rule.** Framing works inherit the eligibility decision of their underlying record (2026-09-29 rule, D313, with the citation priority restated on 2026-09-30). Deck works take their class from the deck reference list's type tag (conference or journal paper → top venue; preprint → institutional supplement, the list having been compiled under the source-credibility rule of 2026-09-27).
6. **Synthesis.** Three classifier agents grouped the records (efficiency central / secondary / absent) into problem-definition families; the document's family sections and cross-family synthesis were written in the main session from the records; the numbers in the document were checked by script against the cited items.

**Counts.** 463 framing records (432 works), all audited: 231 by Claude audit agents and 232 by Codex; 25 rejected as non-paper sources. 438 records passed the audit; 412 of them are eligible under the source rule and appear in the records file (2,472 items), 26 inherit a held or excluded decision and are listed in section 1b. The document cites 89 works (362 items, all audited by Claude agents: 311 corrected, 51 confirmed); E1314–E1675 below are those 362 items in order of first appearance.

**Quotations.** As in the earlier ledgers, this file and the records file give the location (line of the saved text) of every item and no verbatim text of the sources; the anchors stay in the verified store outside the repository.

**Not done.** No experiment. No budget. Slides, dossier and the Codex files unchanged.

### 1c Works with more than one record

Framing records follow the store's records one to one, so a work with several store records (formal version, preprint, supplementary items) has several framing records; the document cites one per work. The groups are those of section 1c of `research/2026-09-29-part3-evidence-ledger.md` and `research/2026-09-30-part3-survey-mining-ledger.md`.

| E1314 | **LLMs are increasingly integrated with external tools, environments and agents, but today's LLM inference systems are designed for standalone LLMs: they treat each external interaction (an 'interception') as the end of generation and form a new request when it finishes. The paper asks how an LLM inference system should efficiently support interceptions.** | Framing statement (how the paper states its problem, challenge, objective, solution or gain); not a measurement. | https://arxiv.org/html/2402.01869v2, v2; line 55 of the saved text; line 55 of the saved text | Thu, 30 May 2024 04:18:03 UTC | Primary, published: Proceedings of the 41st International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML 2024) | P3 record f175#0 | audited: corrected by the auditor, corrected wording shown |
| E1315 | **Inference engines evict a finished request's KV cache when new requests are waiting (end-of-turn eviction); this breaks for agentic workloads that interleave LLM calls with tools, because the tool calls introduce pauses that prevent effective KV reuse across turns, and the paper aims to optimize job completion time for multi-turn agent workloads.** | Framing statement (how the paper states its problem, challenge, objective, solution or gain); not a measurement. | https://arxiv.org/html/2511.02230v7, v7; line 95 of the saved text; line 95 of the saved text | Tue, 8 Sep 2026 19:42:41 UTC | Primary, preprint | P3 record f205#0 | audited: corrected by the auditor, corrected wording shown |
| E1316 | **Agentic programs scale LLM calls and output tokens, but existing LLM serving systems ignore dependencies between programs and calls, missing significant opportunities for optimization (abstract, line 53); because they optimize individual LLM calls without program-level context, they often suffer from suboptimal end-to-end performance for complex programs, in particular programs' end-to-end latencies (line 90). The authors' analysis shows programs submitted to LLM serving engines experience long cumulative wait times, primarily due to head-of-line blocking (line 54).** | Framing statement (how the paper states its problem, challenge, objective, solution or gain); not a measurement. | https://arxiv.org/html/2502.13965v1, v1; line 53 of the saved text; line 53 of the saved text | Wed, 19 Feb 2025 18:59:30 UTC | Primary, published: 23rd USENIX Symposium on Networked Systems Design and Implementation (NSDI 26) | P3 record f218#0 | audited: corrected by the auditor, corrected wording shown |
| E1317 | **LLM applications build workflows from multiple LLM requests but must use the request-level API of public LLM services, which lose application-level information; the services therefore blindly optimize individual requests, giving sub-optimal end-to-end performance for the applications.** | Framing statement (how the paper states its problem, challenge, objective, solution or gain); not a measurement. | https://arxiv.org/html/2405.19888v1, v1; line 75 of the saved text; line 75 of the saved text | Thu, 30 May 2024 09:46:36 UTC | Primary, published: 18th USENIX Symposium on Operating Systems Design and Implementation (OSDI 24) | P3 record f209#0 | audited: corrected by the auditor, corrected wording shown |
| E1318 | **Agentic workflows incur high inference latency because LLMs are invoked repeatedly for each agent; prefix caching of the agents' fixed prompts reduces this, but prefix caching alone is insufficient under limited GPU memory, where the LRU eviction of existing serving systems leads to suboptimal performance in agentic workflows.** | Framing statement (how the paper states its problem, challenge, objective, solution or gain); not a measurement. | https://arxiv.org/html/2507.07400v1, v1; line 77 of the saved text; line 77 of the saved text | Thu, 10 Jul 2025 03:39:23 UTC | Primary, published: Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems 38 (NeurIPS 2025) | P3 record f223#0 | audited: corrected by the auditor, corrected wording shown |
| E1319 | **Existing inference systems treat an interception as the end of a request, so for each interception the initial prompt and all tokens generated before it are formed as a new request, causing substantial recomputation of the KV cache for all previously processed tokens; this recomputation wastes GPU resources that could serve new requests (37-40% of total model forwarding time with the mixed six-augmentation workload, lines 55, 207). The alternatives have their own costs: Preserve avoids recomputation but occupies GPU memory during the entire interception; Swap avoids recomputation, but with limited GPU-CPU link bandwidth foreground forwarding could be bottlenecked waiting for swapping to finish (lines 75-76). Interception time varies drastically across augmentation types and requests, which complicates request scheduling, and context length and interception time also vary highly across requests (lines 69-70).** | Framing statement (how the paper states its problem, challenge, objective, solution or gain); not a measurement. | https://arxiv.org/html/2402.01869v2, v2; line 73 of the saved text; line 73 of the saved text | Thu, 30 May 2024 04:18:03 UTC | Primary, published: Proceedings of the 41st International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML 2024) | P3 record f175#1 | audited: corrected by the auditor, corrected wording shown |
| E1320 | **After eviction the engine must recompute the prefix (prefill) or reload the KV cache from CPU when the tool returns, and, even with CPU offloading, the returning request suffers a per-turn queueing delay waiting for GPU memory, which accumulates over turns and is not measurable by offline profiling; at the same time tool-call durations are variable (some extremely long-tailed), so a retention time must be bounded, since a pinned KV cache can occupy GPU memory for a long time and even cause deadlocks, yet a TTL that expires just before the tool returns wastes the waiting time. Prior work falls short: InferCept decides only on reload cost and does not model per-turn queueing delay or handle variable tool durations, Autellix uses end-of-turn eviction, Pie provides no retention policy, and Ayo/Alto/Parrot assume static workflows.** | Framing statement (how the paper states its problem, challenge, objective, solution or gain); not a measurement. | https://arxiv.org/html/2511.02230v7, v7; line 98 of the saved text; line 98 of the saved text | Tue, 8 Sep 2026 19:42:41 UTC | Primary, preprint | P3 record f205#1 | audited: corrected by the auditor, corrected wording shown |
| E1321 | **Head-of-line blocking at two levels: call-level, where LLM calls with long decodes delay shorter ones (line 132), and program-level, where program-agnostic schedulers let longer programs with many LLM requests delay shorter programs - without program-level context even preemptive MLFQ blindly prioritizes new LLM requests, leading to starvation of shorter programs when long programs' new calls are prioritized (lines 134-135). LLM calls within a program often share common prefixes and cumulative conversation states, while calls across programs typically share only the system prompt (line 96); within a program cache-hit rates remain above 90% across all input lengths, whereas across programs the cache-hit rate decays exponentially with input length, so serving systems across engines should consider a program's data locality to avoid recomputing its KV cache (lines 96, 141). The authors assume a program's LLM invocation pattern emerges only at runtime, making it difficult to fully know or predict the entire graph in advance (line 88).** | Framing statement (how the paper states its problem, challenge, objective, solution or gain); not a measurement. | https://arxiv.org/html/2502.13965v1, v1; line 54 of the saved text; line 54 of the saved text | Wed, 19 Feb 2025 18:59:30 UTC | Primary, published: 23rd USENIX Symposium on Networked Systems Design and Implementation (NSDI 26) | P3 record f218#1 | audited: corrected by the auditor, corrected wording shown |
| E1322 | **Because the service does not understand how requests are correlated, it cannot exploit three opportunities: dependent consecutive requests go back and forth to the client, adding network latency and queuing delays; scheduling optimizes per-request latency rather than end-to-end latency; and common prompt prefixes (e.g. long system prompts, over 94% of tokens repeated in a production search engine) are provided repeatedly in each request, wasting storage, computation and memory bandwidth. The paper adds that without knowledge of the prompt structure, token-by-token matching of every request is expensive at the cluster level, so a public service can hardly detect and co-locate prompt-sharing requests and engine-level redundancy avoidance is hard to bring into effect (line 146).** | Framing statement (how the paper states its problem, challenge, objective, solution or gain); not a measurement. | https://arxiv.org/html/2405.19888v1, v1; line 90 of the saved text; line 90 of the saved text | Thu, 30 May 2024 09:46:36 UTC | Primary, published: 18th USENIX Symposium on Operating Systems Design and Implementation (OSDI 24) | P3 record f209#1 | audited: corrected by the auditor, corrected wording shown |
| E1323 | **LRU eviction fails to anticipate future agent usage and often discards KV caches shortly before their reuse (an agent about to run may have been idle a long time), causing frequent cache misses and recomputation or swapping overhead; memory runs out with many concurrent workflows or very large agent prompts on limited hardware, and reactive CPU-to-GPU loading of offloaded prefixes still adds noticeable transfer latency.** | Framing statement (how the paper states its problem, challenge, objective, solution or gain); not a measurement. | https://arxiv.org/html/2507.07400v1, v1; line 70 of the saved text; line 70 of the saved text | Thu, 10 Jul 2025 03:39:23 UTC | Primary, published: Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems 38 (NeurIPS 2025) | P3 record f223#1 | audited: corrected by the auditor, corrected wording shown |
| E1324 | **Minimize GPU memory waste caused by interceptions ('min-waste interception'; 'minimizing GPU memory waste as a unified objective', line 325). Per-interception waste equations: Eq.1 WasteDiscard_i^j = T_fwd(C_i^j)×C_i^j×M + T_fwd(C_i^j)×C_other×M; Eq.2 WastePreserve_i^j = T_INT^j×C_i×M; Eq.3 WasteSwap_i^j = 2×T_swap(C_i^j)×C_batch×M; Eq.4 WasteChunkD_i^j = T_fwd(C_i^j)×C_i^j×M/2 + n×T_fwd(C_i^j/n)×C_other×M; Eq.5 Waste_i^j = min(WastePreserve_i^j, WasteChunkD_i^j), used to rank intercepted requests. Symbols (from the text): i = request, j = interception; C_i^j = context tokens of request i when interception j occurs; C_i in Eq.2 = the request's context held in GPU memory (line 209); M = KV-cache memory per token; T_fwd = mapping from number of scheduled tokens in a batch to that iteration's execution time; C_other = sum of the contexts of other requests; T_INT^j = duration of interception j (for highly variable or not offline-profiled interceptions estimated as T̂_INT = t_now - t_call, line 282); T_swap = mapping from number of tokens to swap to swapping latency; C_batch appears in Eq.3 without a separate definition; n = number of iterations to finish recomputing the request. Swap limit per iteration: T_swap(N_i) = T_fwd(B_i), where N_i = number of tokens that can be swapped for free (hidden behind forwarding) at iteration i with batch size B_i (line 234); recomputation chunk size = S minus the running group size, S = GPU saturation point in query tokens (lines 247-248). Swap-in/out budgets are set by maximizing inference throughput (tokens added to an iteration) under three memory/bandwidth constraints (lines 238-241). Reported evaluation metrics: normalized latency vs request rate (intercepted time removed from end-to-end latency), completed requests per second, TTFT.** | Framing statement (how the paper states its problem, challenge, objective, solution or gain); not a measurement. | https://arxiv.org/html/2402.01869v2, v2; line 80 of the saved text; line 80 of the saved text | Thu, 30 May 2024 04:18:03 UTC | Primary, published: Proceedings of the 41st International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML 2024) | P3 record f175#2 | audited: corrected by the auditor, corrected wording shown |
| E1325 | **TTL choice maximizes expected net benefit in units of time (total job completion latency): τ* = argmax_τ P(τ,f) × Benefit(r) − Cost(τ,r) (Eq. 1), with Cost(τ,r) = MemUsage(r)/M × τ; Benefit(r) = CacheMissCost(r) + OutofOrderCost(r); CacheMissCost(r) = MemUsage(r) × Prefill-Reload(r) / M; OutofOrderCost(r) = T/M × MemUsage(r) × η; η = −Corr(k, N−k); P(τ,f) = (1//S[f]/) Σ_{t∈S[f]} I[t ≤ τ]; after cancelling MemUsage(r)/M this becomes argmax_τ P(τ,f) × (T·η + Prefill-Reload(r)) − τ (Eq. 2). Symbols: r a request that generates tool call f; τ the TTL (seconds); MemUsage(r) GPU memory of r's KV cache; M average GPU memory footprint of active requests; Prefill-Reload(r) time to reconstruct the KV cache by prefill or CPU reload (from offline profiling); T average queueing delay of evicted requests (sliding-window average); η memoryfulness factor of the workload, with N the total requests in a program and k those already served; S[f] historical durations of tool f; I the indicator function. Reported end-to-end targets are average job completion time / delay and throughput.** | Framing statement (how the paper states its problem, challenge, objective, solution or gain); not a measurement. | https://arxiv.org/html/2511.02230v7, v7; line 479 of the saved text; line 479 of the saved text | Tue, 8 Sep 2026 19:42:41 UTC | Primary, preprint | P3 record f205#2 | audited: corrected by the auditor, corrected wording shown |
| E1326 | **Minimize programs' end-to-end latency (response time), which for a single-threaded program comprises (1) waiting time (total queuing time of its LLM calls on the engine), (2) execution time (cumulative feedforward time of its LLM calls) and (3) interceptions (time waiting for external interrupts such as tool calls or human input); Autellix targets (1) and (2) because (3) is unrelated to LLM serving (line 124). Priority formulas: PLAS, Eq. 1, p(c_j) = sum of runtimes t_k of all prior LLM calls k<j with the same program ID (larger value = lower priority, lines 254-260); ATLAS, Eq. 2, p(c_j) = 0 if c_j is a root, otherwise max over parents c_k of (p(c_k) + t_k), an estimate of the critical path (lines 262-268). Anti-starvation promotes a call to Q_1 when W_total / T_total >= beta (line 276-278). Stated system objectives: (1) program end-to-end latency for users, (2) maximizing GPU utilization for providers, (3) mitigating starvation / fairness measured via P95 and P99 latencies (line 147). Evaluation metric: program-level token latency = total program response time / number of tokens generated (critical-path response time / total tokens across threads for multi-threaded programs), with throughput compared at equal latency (lines 353-355).** | Framing statement (how the paper states its problem, challenge, objective, solution or gain); not a measurement. | https://arxiv.org/html/2502.13965v1, v1; line 124 of the saved text; line 124 of the saved text | Wed, 19 Feb 2025 18:59:30 UTC | Primary, published: 23rd USENIX Symposium on Networked Systems Design and Implementation (NSDI 26) | P3 record f218#2 | audited: corrected by the auditor, corrected wording shown |
| E1327 | **The scheduler's primary goal is to meet the varied performance goals of LLM applications while optimizing GPU cluster utilization (line 208): applications annotate final output Semantic Variables with a performance criterion, end-to-end latency or throughput (line 183), e.g. minimise the end-to-end time to receive the final summary (line 140) or to deliver the final code (line 341). Requests generating a throughput-annotated variable are marked throughput-preferred when scheduling (line 200); for latency-critical outputs, parallel requests at the same stage form a task group whose latency as a whole the scheduler should minimise, often with a higher batch capacity (line 201); to meet latency targets an engine must keep its token count below a threshold set by the request with the strictest latency constraint (line 208). | E1328 | **InferCept derives waste equations for Discard, Preserve and Swap; reduces the waste of Swap (layer-by-layer swap pipelining overlapped with model forwarding, and swap chunking across iterations so each iteration stays within a swap budget the GPU-CPU link can sustain) and of Discard (recomputation split into chunks small enough to fit the GPU processing capacity left unused by decoding); and sorts intercepted requests in descending order of potential memory waste (Eq. 5), swaps out context from them in that order until the swap-out budget (determined at every iteration, lines 236-241) runs out, and preserves or discards the remaining requests according to the Eq. 5 decision; for resumption, it assigns the swap-in budget to swapped-out requests with the earliest original arrival times and schedules discarded, preserved and non-intercepted waiting requests in FCFS order until the GPU's processing capacity (saturation point) is reached, FCFS ensuring fairness (lines 82-93, 268-278).** | Framing statement (how the paper states its problem, challenge, objective, solution or gain); not a measurement. | https://arxiv.org/html/2402.01869v2, v2; line 89 of the saved text; line 89 of the saved text | Thu, 30 May 2024 04:18:03 UTC | Primary, published: Proceedings of the 41st International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML 2024) | P3 record f175#3 | audited: corrected by the auditor, corrected wording shown |

| E1338 | **Unlike server-based, conversation-oriented LLM workloads whose latency is dominated by decode, on-device agents spend significant time in both prefill and decode: agents process much longer inputs than they generate, and on-device accelerators offer limited compute throughput and memory bandwidth, rendering prefill disproportionately expensive; dynamically retrieved tool descriptions and guidelines inserted early in the Planner prompt limit KV cache reuse by prefix caching; decode output is highly correlated with the provided examples and user prompt (96% of Planner and 87% of Arbiter output tokens overlap with their input prompt), so a large portion of decode time is spent generating straightforward, formulaic sequences that do not require the LLM's reasoning capabilities; and speculative decoding is hindered on-device by draft-LLM latency and the 'multi-token tax' of multi-token verification.** | Framing statement (how the paper states its problem, challenge, objective, solution or gain); not a measurement. | https://arxiv.org/html/2605.10380v1, v1; line 76 of the saved text; line 76 of the saved text | Mon, 11 May 2026 11:23:38 UTC | Primary, published: Proceedings of the 24th Annual International Conference on Mobile Systems, Applications a… | P3 record f183#1 | audited: corrected by the auditor, corrected wording shown |
| E1339 | **Generation latency can be a dominant bottleneck: on ToolBench with Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct, generating tool calls accounts for about 80% of end-to-end inference latency and is roughly 4x the tool execution time; tool execution time is often relatively stable for a fixed toolset and environment, while tool-calling generation latency grows with model size and the length of the generated tool-call sequence. | E1350 | **Most agent frameworks allocate frontier-model compute uniformly, invoking the same large multimodal model at every interaction, although GUI trajectories are heterogeneous: many steps are routine and can be executed reliably by much smaller models, while failures concentrate in a small number of high-risk moments (progress stalls and silent semantic drift). Query-level cascades are not enough: at the step level the controller must judge whether an ongoing interaction is still on track, intermediate actions are weak proxies for correctness, verifying every step with a stronger model would erase much of the efficiency benefit, and routing must stay stable (avoid thrashing between models) while still enabling timely recovery.** | Framing statement (how the paper states its problem, challenge, objective, solution or gain); not a measurement. | https://arxiv.org/html/2604.27151v1, v1; line 83 of the saved text; line 83 of the saved text | Wed, 29 Apr 2026 19:59:36 UTC | Primary, preprint | P3 record f233#1 | audited: corrected by the auditor, corrected wording shown |
| E1351 | **Thought generation consumes the majority of inference compute per step, and in reasoning models thinking may continue well beyond the point where the intended action has converged (prior work shows the committed action is encoded in hidden representations well before the trace ends), implying many post-convergence tokens are waste; excessive thinking may also make the edge model increasingly overconfident in a suboptimal action, which complicates cloud-deferral mechanisms that rely on the edge model's internal uncertainty, while early truncation may raise uncertainty above the model's actual confidence and unduly increase deferral. Even a well-reasoned edge action can be wrong and errors compound over the episode. Related work (Section 2) states that thought calibration, early-exit methods and conformal-prediction wrappers do not jointly calibrate a stopping rule with a deferral mechanism under formal statistical guarantees, that model cascades route single-turn queries without multi-step dependencies, and that ReDAct applies PPL-guided deferral inside an agentic loop but chooses its deferral threshold heuristically.** | Framing statement (how the paper states its problem, challenge, objective, solution or gain); not a measurement. | https://arxiv.org/html/2607.26865v3, v3; line 130 of the saved text; line 130 of the saved text | Fri, 25 Sep 2026 15:00:53 UTC | Primary, preprint | P3 record f188#1 | audited: corrected by the auditor, corrected wording shown |
| E1352 | **A large number of reasoning tokens accumulate at each step along multi-step trajectories, while not all steps require the same level of thinking (line 89), so a fixed, static effort strategy often fails to balance performance and cost; a slight reasoning deficit in an early step can lead to error propagation (line 90); for routing across different models the performance-cost trade-off is not always predictable or monotonic and switching models means re-encoding context (line 90); and for training a router the optimal effort per step cannot be observed directly, with naive trial-and-error prohibitive over an /E/^T search space (line 134).** | Framing statement (how the paper states its problem, challenge, objective, solution or gain); not a measurement. | https://arxiv.org/html/2603.07915v1, v1; line 87 of the saved text; line 87 of the saved text | Mon, 9 Mar 2026 03:17:29 UTC | Primary, preprint | P3 record f243#1 | audited: corrected by the auditor, corrected wording shown |

E[Σ_{i=1}^{z} c̃_{L_i,2}//f_{L_i}(q)// + c̃_{L_i,1}//q// + c̃_{L_i,0}] ≤ b, z = argmin_i g(q, f_{L_i}(q)) ≥ τ_i, where L ∈ [K]^m are the indexes of the m APIs selected by the router, τ the threshold vector, g(·,·): Q×A→[0,1] the generation scoring function giving a reliability score, and z the API at which the router stops and returns the answer. | E1357 | **Soft-budget routing (Eq. 1): J_soft(θ) = E_{τ~π_θ}[ I(success(τ)) − λ Σ_{t=0}^{/τ/−1} c(a_t) ], where I(success(τ)) ∈ {0,1} is a sparse terminal indicator of task success, λ >= 0 is a Lagrange multiplier whose variation traces the efficiency frontier, a_t ∈ {M_small, M_large} is the model chosen at step t from history state s_t, and c(a_t) > 0 is its scalar cost. Hard-budget routing (Eq. 2, a CMDP): max_π E_{τ~π}[I(success(τ))] s.t. Σ_{t} c(a_t) <= B_max, with B_max the maximum allowable per-task budget (e.g. API cost limit or number of large-model calls). The RL training reward (Eq. 5) is R(τ,x) = (r_success + r_hard) − λ·C_norm(τ,x), with r_hard a bonus only for Hard tasks and C_norm = clip((C(τ) − C_min(x)) / (C_max(x) − C_min(x) + ε), 0, 1), where C(τ) = Σ_t c(a_t) and C_min, C_max are the min and max of the always-small and always-large trajectory costs. Hard-task SFT expert (Eqs. 3-4): sample N trajectories with P(a_t = M_large) = p_k = k/N and take τ* = argmin over successful trajectories of Σ_t c(a_t). BoPO advantage (Eq. 7): A(τ_g) = (R(τ_g,x) − max(μ_group, R(τ*,x))) / (σ_group + ε); policy objective (Eq. 8): J_BoPO(θ) = E[(1/G) Σ_g (π_θ(τ_g)/π_old(τ_g)·A(τ_g) − β_KL·D_KL(π_θ // π_ref))], π_ref the SFT model (G = 8, β = 0.04, r_success = 1.0, r_hard = 0.5, λ ∈ {0.1,0.3,0.5,0.7,0.9}; Appendix D). Budget-Constrained Decoding (Eq. 9): with residual budget b_t = B_max − Σ_{i<t} c(a_i), force M_small if c(M_large) >= b_t, else follow π_θ; in experiments B_max is the number K ∈ {5,10,15} of large-model calls. | E1366 | **A lightweight router reads the interaction history and current observation and predicts the lowest appropriate reasoning-effort level of the same agent model for the next step, so high effort is reserved for difficult steps; it is trained on labels from a pipeline that fixes a successful concise trajectory and finds, step by step, the minimum effort that reproduces the correct action (with a teacher-written 3-5-sentence rationale), then further trained with GRPO; using one model's own thinking levels gives a more well-defined and consistent performance-cost frontier and lets the agent reuse the KV cache across effort levels.** | Framing statement (how the paper states its problem, challenge, objective, solution or gain); not a measurement. | https://arxiv.org/html/2603.07915v1, v1; line 91 of the saved text; line 91 of the saved text | Mon, 9 Mar 2026 03:17:29 UTC | Primary, preprint | P3 record f243#3 | audited: corrected by the auditor, corrected wording shown |
| E1367 | **A lightweight convergence probe on the edge model's hidden states halts on-device reasoning once the intended action has stabilized, a perplexity-based deferral rule escalates uncertain actions to the cloud model, and the two thresholds are jointly calibrated on end-to-end episode trajectories with multi-objective Learn-Then-Test, giving finite-sample guarantees on expected episode reward and cloud-call rate. | E1383 | **Each tool result conditions all subsequent tokens, so the cycle enforces a strict serial dependency: the tool call cannot be dispatched until decoding completes and the next decode cannot begin until the result arrives; tool wait is bounded by network round-trips, container cold-starts and database I/O, and as model inference gets faster its share of wall time grows. Token-level speculative decoding and serving systems do not dispatch tools earlier, and agents generate tool calls online, so there is no static graph to prefetch.** | Framing statement (how the paper states its problem, challenge, objective, solution or gain); not a measurement. | https://arxiv.org/html/2607.03333v1, v1; line 240 of the saved text; line 240 of the saved text | Fri, 3 Jul 2026 13:51:32 UTC | Primary, preprint | P3 record f178#1 | audited: corrected by the auditor, corrected wording shown |
| E1384 | **Each tool call forces generation to stop and return to the user, an interrupt-driven process with a strict sequential dependency in which tool latencies accumulate; evicted sequences and tool output must be rescheduled back into the engine (and the history prefilled again), and prefix caching does not remove the overheads of removing, processing and rescheduling sequences, which worsen in multi-tenant settings; breaking this is non-trivial because tool latencies span orders of magnitude and are hard to predict or bound, long tools dominate end-to-end latency while for short tools eviction and re-entry overheads can outweigh latency hiding, running tools early or out of order risks wasted computation or outputs inconsistent with the model's eventual decisions, and existing in-engine optimizations (speculative decoding, KV-cache management) treat tools as opaque interruptions.** | Framing statement (how the paper states its problem, challenge, objective, solution or gain); not a measurement. | https://arxiv.org/html/2512.15834v1, v1; line 86 of the saved text; line 86 of the saved text | Wed, 17 Dec 2025 18:22:44 UTC | Primary, preprint | P3 record f221#1 | audited: corrected by the auditor, corrected wording shown |

| E1407 | **Most GUI agents execute long-horizon tasks as a serial chain of perception–decision–action cycles, each incurring a costly LMM inference; the paper cites Abhyankar et al. Model- and system-level acceleration (quantization, pruning, distillation) lowers per-call latency but leaves the serial loop untouched (line 106). | E1411 | **First, high latency and long response time: each individual UI interaction step requires one LLM call to reason which UI to interact with, so a task involving multiple UI interaction steps can incur considerable time and monetary costs; LLM call latency is also positively correlated with the number of processed tokens, and to get high-quality outputs the UI agent must pass a large volume of UI information to describe the current state, which also increases latency in each call (lines 51-72). Second, reliability: during long sequential calls the chance of selecting a wrong UI control or hallucinating a non-existent UI increases with each reasoning step, resulting in compounding errors and task failure (lines 73-84). Third, UI generalization: how UI agents handle applications whose UIs are not included in LLM pretraining remains a critical obstacle without good solutions (lines 84-91).** | Framing statement (how the paper states its problem, challenge, objective, solution or gain); not a measurement. | https://aclanthology.org/2025.acl-long.381.pdf, pdf; line 53 of the saved text; line 53 of the saved text |  | Primary, published: Proceedings of the 63rd Annual Meeting of the Association for Computational Linguistics (… | P3 record f215#1 | audited: corrected by the auditor, corrected wording shown |

| E1418 | **Three limitations of prior approaches: a static tool set of primitive actions (the minimum spanning set to reach any browser state) that costs longer execution times and higher error rates; sequential execution that does not explore the space of schedules such as task parallelism and request hedging; and non-determinism post-plan, where the standard agent loop calls the LM at each step even when no LM call is needed between tool calls; in addition, prior code-action work ignores that many valid code plans exist with different latency.** | Framing statement (how the paper states its problem, challenge, objective, solution or gain); not a measurement. | https://arxiv.org/html/2605.21470v2, v2; line 109 of the saved text; line 109 of the saved text | Fri, 29 May 2026 17:54:05 UTC | Primary, published: Proceedings of the 43rd International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML 2026) | P3 record f234#1 | audited: corrected by the auditor, corrected wording shown |
| E1419 | **The overheads trace to three off-the-shelf components (frontier LLMs, browsers, ReAct planning) applied to every step of every task, which fail to exploit the web's step heterogeneity (most steps are mechanical) and task-driven structure (stable URL templates, layouts and answer formats). Automating shortcuts is hard: naively substituting cheap components into the ReAct loop drops average task success by 60%, because cheap models struggle to choose among hundreds of interactive elements, direct-URL fetching requires knowing in advance which page holds the answer, and errors compound across a trajectory (§2.3 names C1: determining what can be specialized; C2: determining how to specialize across steps, i.e., the minimum sufficient tier per step without paying the cost of running the default agent to find out).** | Framing statement (how the paper states its problem, challenge, objective, solution or gain); not a measurement. | https://arxiv.org/html/2605.16565v2, v2; line 56 of the saved text; line 56 of the saved text | Tue, 19 May 2026 13:45:27 UTC | Primary, published: Proceedings of the European Conference on Computer Systems (EuroSys 2027) | P3 record f235#1 | audited: corrected by the auditor, corrected wording shown |

| E1423 | **Two argmin objectives. Planner (Algorithm 1): among k = 32 valid candidate plans, return argmin over (pi, c) of c, where c is a unitless weighted call count that accumulates C_tool * gamma^d for each tool call and C_eval * gamma^d for each ai_eval (LLM) call at CFG depth d (base weights C_tool = 0.1, C_eval = 10.0; gamma = 10 is a loop-nesting penalty), and a plan is invalid if the tracked state s does not contain a tool's precondition (s is updated with the tool's postconditions); the paper states this cost only ranks plans by expected latency (by number of LLM calls and nesting depth) and does not predict absolute latency. Scheduler (Algorithm 2): minimizes estimated latency. Scheduler (Algorithm 2): return argmin over sigma in {Ser, Par, Hed} of mean latency L-bar_sigma over N_MC Monte Carlo trials, with serial latency S(U_sigma), hedge latency min_{w=1..n} S(U_sigma) + delta_h, and parallel latency S(U_sigma^seq) + max_{w=1..n} S_w(U_sigma,w) + delta_p, where S(U) = sum_{e in U} sum_{i=1}^{count(e)} sample(D_e), U_sigma is the LLM-predicted element usage, count(e) the predicted number of interactions with element e, D_e its learned latency distribution, n the workers (vCPU budget, n = 4 in experiments), N_MC = 1000, and delta_h (5-15 s), delta_p (20-30 s) overheads. Reported metrics: benchmark-harness accuracy (REAL state-diff checking; WebArena evaluation functions) and mean wall-clock latency from task submission to completion including planning overhead, over 3 runs; planning efficiency is also measured by Pass@k = 1 - C(n-c,k)/C(n,k) and Pass@t = 1 - (1 - F(t) p)^{n_parallel}, with F(t) the latency CDF and p = c/n.** | Framing statement (how the paper states its problem, challenge, objective, solution or gain); not a measurement. | https://arxiv.org/html/2605.21470v2, v2; line 284 of the saved text; line 284 of the saved text | Fri, 29 May 2026 17:54:05 UTC | Primary, published: Proceedings of the 43rd International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML 2026) | P3 record f234#2 | audited: corrected by the auditor, corrected wording shown |

| E1455 | **The root cause is the ever-growing trajectory: once the LLM calls a tool, the call and its result stay in the trajectory until the task ends and are included in every subsequent input, so verbose outputs snowball in cost (the paper cites that in September 2025, 99% of Claude 4 Sonnet's daily tokens on OpenRouter were input tokens accumulated in the trajectory); trajectories contain useless, redundant and expired information. Existing token-reduction work targets single-turn tasks, reducing all input tokens at once (so the timing of reduction is an open question for agents), working on natural language rather than structured code, or requiring changes to LLM inference or the model; agent products compact only when the context window is full or the user asks, aiming at robustness rather than efficiency. KV Cache does not remove the need and makes reduction harder, since modifying a token invalidates the cache for all later tokens, and LLM agents given an erase tool often did not reduce their own trajectories despite explicit prompts.** | Framing statement (how the paper states its problem, challenge, objective, solution or gain); not a measurement. | https://arxiv.org/html/2509.23586v2, v2; line 94 of the saved text; line 94 of the saved text | Sun, 15 Mar 2026 11:20:36 UTC | Primary, published: Proceedings of the ACM on Software Engineering, 3 | P3 record f238#1 | audited: corrected by the auditor, corrected wording shown |

| E1510 | **Agents have amnesia because most LLMs are stateless; they lack systematic mechanisms to learn from their execution experiences (line 74). Extracting actionable learnings from trajectories is non-trivial: valuable patterns exist across clean successes, inefficient successes, failure-then-recovery sequences and complete failures; causality is often non-obvious from raw logs (a failure at step 15 may stem from a decision at step 3); and learnings must be contextually retrieved, actionable and specific, and traceable to their source (lines 92-97). Existing approaches fall short (lines 107-111): rule-based systems cannot adapt to unforeseen situations and need constant manual maintenance; prompt engineering is generic and has no mechanism for automatic improvement from observed outcomes; generic memory systems (Mem0, Letta) lack understanding of execution patterns, causal analysis, structured learning extraction and provenance; RL needs extensive training data, is computationally expensive to train and update, and offers limited interpretability.** | Framing statement (how the paper states its problem, challenge, objective, solution or gain); not a measurement. | https://arxiv.org/html/2603.10600v1, v1 (the only version listed); line 74 of the saved text; line 74 of the saved text | 2026-03-11 | Primary, preprint | P3 record f068#1 | audited: corrected by the auditor, corrected wording shown |

## 4 Still open

- **Decision for Edwin: the mixed audit.** 231 records were audited by Claude agents and 232 by Codex (gpt-6-astra) under the same rules; whether a Claude spot-check of the Codex batch is wanted (it costs Claude quota).
- **Decision for Edwin: held works.** Revisiting Observation Reduction (h033) and FRAMES (n007) are held under the institution test; the document describes them without citing them (its section 8).
- **Non-paper sources.** Codex rejected 25 framing records whose source is not a research paper (blog posts, documentation, READMEs, pricing pages), including Autobrowse (w032) and "Improving Deep Agents with harness engineering" (w029), which the document describes without citing; their framing records stay in the store.
- **Vendor materials.** 16 deck items (pricing pages, product documentation, telemetry posts) were not given framing records; the classifier flagged two store records of the same kind (n018, w027) as stating no problem.
- **No second classification.** The family taxonomy comes from three classifier agents (one per efficiency group) and the main session; a second independent classification was not run (quota). The family assignment of every record is in the store's `framing-synth/` files.
- **Duplicate records.** 463 records cover 432 works: formal and preprint versions and supplementary items of the same work have separate framing records (e.g. WALT h007/h008/x012, AWM h004/h005/x010); the document cites one per work.
- **Merging.** E-entries of this file are proposed records for v3.1. The canonical v3 is unchanged.
